=== READER SYNOPSIS (60 reviews, avg 76.2/100) ===
# Reader Feedback Summary

**Readers say** — This corporate-dystopian thriller earns a solid but not enthusiastic reception: most readers landed in the competent-but-frustrating range, with a meaningful minority genuinely unsettled by it. The procedural dread and bureaucratic precision are the story's clearest strengths — readers consistently responded to the way institutional language ("priority weighting," thermopaper ciphers, badge declines) does the horror's heavy lifting without melodrama. The central problem, raised repeatedly across the score range, is that the story is emotionally withheld to a fault: Rhea moves through eleven days of complicity and danger with the affect of a supply-chain report, and the moments that should land as gut-punches — particularly the footnote reveal in Beat 11 — arrive with clinical distance instead of visceral weight. Several readers flagged the middle section as wheel-spinning, especially the bank, cipher, and transit-transfer sequence, which reads more like a procedural checklist than a scene with a person in it. The ending drew consistent dissatisfaction: as at least one reader put it, the story needs "a held breath, not a pin drop," and the current close feels like a setup for a sequel rather than a payoff. At least one reader also noted that the "fourteen-pixel" redaction bar feels anachronistically 2020s-digital for a 2217 physical-document world — a small but telling sign that the worldbuilding's specificity occasionally misfires. The bones are genuinely admired; the flesh is what's missing.

---

**What landed**
- The bureaucratic prose register mirrors the story's thematic content — language as complicity is structurally enacted, not just described
- "Priority weighting" and the OPTIC-7 footnote are widely praised as models of restrained, chilling revelation
- The procedural worldbuilding (thermopaper, transit slugs, badge systems) feels lived-in and earned without over-explanation
- Beat structure gives the piece a clean, audio-friendly rhythm that several readers appreciated
- Rhea's non-heroic interiority — a cog who found the wrong memo — reads as psychologically credible

---

**Top fixes (most-requested first)**

- **Pacing / prose — Beat 11 (footnote reveal):** Add a physiological beat before the clinical dosage detail — a trembling hand, the air going cold — so the horror lands in the body before the brain. Multiple readers independently flagged this as the story's most under-delivered moment.
- **Pacing — Beats 14–15 (breath-counting):** Cut the repeated "she counted her breaths" ritual by half; one instance establishes the pattern, two kills momentum.
- **Pacing / clarity — Beats 19–21 (bank/cipher/transit sequence):** Trim aggressively — cut the Andean Remnants journalist name-drop, condense to one transit transfer, and use the recovered space to show Rhea's hands shaking or not shaking while she copies the document.
- **Characters — Marcus (Beats 22 & 28):** Either give him one earlier micro-presence so his cold-coffee callback in Beat 28 resonates as pattern, or cut him and let the director's repeated "exceptional work" carry the irony alone.
- **Ending — Beat 33:** Replace "She went in" with a concrete sensory anchor — a door closing, a reflection in glass, the hum of ventilation — to close an emotional loop rather than trailing off into sequel-bait.
- **Prose — Beat 16:** "She wrote four paragraphs that would not have shamed her at Tessera" is too abstract; give one specific, horrible sentence she actually wrote to make the moral compromise concrete.
- **Clarity — Beats 18–19 (timeline jump):** Add a simple temporal marker ("Four days earlier…") to prevent listener disorientation when the narrative breaks its established linearity.
- **Characters — Joaquim (Beats 3–4):** Condense his setup to a single tense moment of Rhea reading the clipboard; his habits don't need spelling out, and at least one reader noted his carelessness feels too convenient as written.
- **Prose / worldbuilding — "fourteen-pixel" (Beat 6):** Replace with tactile language ("a redaction bar the width of a thumb") to match the physical-document context of 2217.
- **Characters — Mira Quintero:** Give Rhea one prior memory of Mira — even imagined — so "priority weighting" registers as a person rather than a policy term.

---

**The split** — Readers who scored it 81–100 were responding to the structural irony and tonal control — the story's *idea* working at a high level; readers in the 61–72 range felt that same control had become a liability, producing a protagonist whose danger and complicity they were told about but never felt.
